<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2025 Jan 17; Accepted 2025 Nov 30; Collection date 2025 Dec.</p></section></div></div>

<p>This is an open access article distributed under the terms of the <a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Creative Commons Attribution License</a>, which permits unrestricted use, distribution, and reproduction in any medium, provided the original author and source are credited.</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC12721539  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41396985/" class="usa-link">41396985</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>The subcellular localisation of proteins is a key determinant of their function. High-throughput analyses of these localisations can be performed using mass spectrometry-based spatial proteomics, which enables us to examine the localisation and relocalisation of proteins. Furthermore, complementary data sources can provide additional sources of functional or localisation information. Examples include protein annotations and other high-throughput ‘omic assays. Integrating these modalities can provide new insights as well as additional confidence in results, but existing approaches for integrative analyses of spatial proteomics datasets, such as concatenation-based methods and transfer learning approaches like KNN-TL, are limited in the types of data they can integrate and do not quantify uncertainty in their predictions. Here we propose a semi-supervised Bayesian approach (wherein model parameters are inferred from <em>both</em> labeled marker proteins and unlabeled data while quantifying prediction uncertainty) to integrate spatial proteomics datasets with other data sources, to improve the inference of protein sub-cellular localisation. We demonstrate our approach outperforms other transfer-learning methods and has greater flexibility in the data it can model - including categorical annotations (e.g., Gene Ontology terms), continuous measurements (e.g., protein abundance), and temporal profiles (e.g., time-series expression data). To demonstrate the flexibility of our approach, we apply our method to integrate spatial proteomics data generated for the parasite <em>Toxoplasma gondii</em> with time-series gene expression data generated over its cell cycle. Our findings suggest that proteins linked to invasion organelles are associated with expression programs that peak at the end of the first cell-cycle. Furthermore, this integrative analysis divides the dense granule proteins into heterogeneous populations suggestive of potentially different functions. Our method is disseminated via the <kbd>mdir</kbd> R package available on the lead author’s Github.</p></section><section class="abstract" id="abstract2"><h2>Author summary</h2>
<p>Proteins are located in subcellular environments to ensure that they are near their interaction partners and occur in the correct biochemical environment to function. Where a protein is located can be determined from a number of data sources. To integrate diverse datasets together we develop an integrative Bayesian model to combine the information from several datasets in a principled manner. We learn how similar the dataset are as part of the modelling process and demonstrate the benefits of integrating mass-spectrometry based spatial proteomics data with timecourse gene-expression datasets.</p></section><section id="sec001"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p>Proteins are key to all cellular processes including cell proliferation and survival. To function correctly, a protein must interact with other binding partners and substrates which requires them to localise to the correct subcellular compartment [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref001" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref001">1</a>]. For example, signalling and metabolic pathways are dependent on the location of the constituent proteins [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref002" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref002">2</a>]. Mislocation to the incorrect compartment is increasingly associated with disease, including cancer and obesity [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref003" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref003">3</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref007" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref007">7</a>]. Thus, understanding the location of proteins within the cell is of use in the development of therapeutic targets and determining disease aetiology [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref008" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref008">8</a>]. Mass-spectrometry (<strong>MS</strong>) based spatial proteomics can be used to study the localisation and relocalisation of proteins on a proteome- and system-wide scale. These experiments leverage so-called marker proteins with an unambiguous single localisation and then apply machine learning approaches, such as the support vector machine (<strong>SVM</strong>), to predict the localisation of annotated proteins.</p>
<p>In a typical MS-based spatial proteomics experiment cells are first gently lysed, to dissociate the cellular compartments while preserving organelle integrity. This cellular material is then separated into subcellular fractions using one of a range of methods such as differential centrifugation [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref009" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref009">9</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref010" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref010">10</a>] or equilibrium density centrifugation [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref011" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref011">11</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref012" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref012">12</a>]. Following subcellular fractionation, the proteins are digested to peptides and subject to high accuracy quantitative MS generating a signature of abundance across the subcellular fractions which correlates with subcellular localisation. A wide variety of methods exploit these central principles, including protein correlation profiling, SubCellBarCode, dynamic organeller maps amongst others [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref009" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref009">9</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref011" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref011">11</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref013" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref013">13</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref015" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref015">15</a>]. In the localisation of organelle proteins by isotope tagging (<strong>LOPIT</strong>) [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref011" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref011">11</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref016" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref016">16</a>] and hyperplexed LOPIT (<strong>hyper-LOPIT</strong>) [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref012" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref012">12</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref017" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref017">17</a>] approaches, cell lysis is preceded by the separation of subcellular components along a continuous density gradient. Discrete fractions along this gradient are then collected and multiplexed quantification is achieved using tandem mass tags (<strong>TMT</strong>) [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref018" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref018">18</a>]. Protein distributions revealing organelle specific profiles within the fractions are acquired by using synchronous precursor selection mass-spectrometry (<strong>SPS-MS3</strong>). Proteins belonging to the same organelle possess shared quantitative abundance profiles [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref019" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref019">19</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref020">20</a>], it is these (normalised) abundance profiles that are the input to machine learning algorithms to predict the localisation of the unannotated proteins. This means the marker proteins form a labelled subset of the data which can be used as a training set for supervised methods (e.g., partial least squares discriminant analysis [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref016" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref016">16</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref021" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref021">21</a>]; the support vector machine [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref009" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref009">9</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref012" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref012">12</a>]). Using a supervised method assumes that both the marker proteins are fully representative of their compartment, that all compartments have known marker proteins and that all subcellular niches are observed in the labelled set. Supervised classification remains the most common type of Machine Learning approach to prediction tasks in proteomics [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref022" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref022">22</a>]. If we do not believe these assumptions hold, we can employ semi-supervised methods to both infer the unobserved localisations and update the parameters describing the components simultaneously [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref023" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref023">23</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref024" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref024">24</a>]. These methods can typical uncover unannotated subcellular niches and have better accuracy at inferring the underlying model parameters.</p>
<p>‘Omic technologies that infer, say, gene expression, report on different properties of proteins that can help us to understand the aetiology of diseases and conditions that cause functional changes in the system, e.g., cancers, neurodegenerative diseases and immune-mediated diseases, etc. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref025" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref025">25</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref030">30</a>]. Deepening understanding of gene expression might elucidate the mechanisms by which viruses and some parasites hijack host organelles to replicate [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref031" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref031">31</a>]. Additionally they can have implication for disease diagnosis and therapeutic success [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref032" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref032">32</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref033" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref033">33</a>]. More generally, ‘omic measurements can give us an integrated view on the molecular mechanisms of biological processes [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref034" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref034">34</a>]. However, integrating diverse datasets (sometimes referred to as modalities in the machine learning literature) is challenging because the different measurement processes generate heterogeneous data structures and each method has its own set of limitations. Current integrative approaches have significant limitations for spatial proteomics applications. Simply concatenating the datasets assumes each dataset contributes equal information towards inference and ignores dataset specific variability.</p>
<p>Despite the successes of multi-omic data integration [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref035">35</a>], most methods are unsupervised and tend to infer latent factors that explain variation in the data; that is, a de-convolution of the dataset into a low-dimensional representation [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref036" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref036">36</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref037" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref037">37</a>] or to identify shared clusters [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref038" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref038">38</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref040">40</a>] but cannot leverage the valuable marker protein annotations available in spatial proteomics experiments. Transfer learning approaches like k-nearest neighbors [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref041">41</a>] can incorporate auxiliary data but provide point estimates without uncertainty quantification and are limited to pairwise dataset comparisons.</p>
<p>Bayesian methods are particularly well-suited to address these limitations because they provide a principled framework for: (1) incorporating prior biological knowledge through marker proteins in a semi-supervised setting, (2) quantifying uncertainty in protein localisation predictions, (3) learning the degree of information sharing between datasets rather than assuming it, and (4) handling heterogeneous data types through modality-specific probability models [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref042" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref042">42</a>]. Here, we perform integrative analysis of MS-based spatial proteomics data with other datasets (e.g. Gene Ontology (<strong>GO</strong>) annotations and Gene-expression datasets) as they contain powerful signals that may uncover subcellular regulation of processes that are not apparent from considering another dataset in isolation. Furthermore, the signal in one MS-based spatial proteomics dataset may be boosted by considering multiple datasets simultaneously. More generally, we wish to jointly model structure in across datasets and uncover shared clusters, groups or regulation while properly accounting for uncertainty and dataset-specific characteristics.</p>
<p>When considering pairs of datasets (though we can consider larger combinations), we can have one of the following three scenarios. Either both our modalities have some observed classes, neither do, or one modality does. MS-based spatial proteomic data are annotated with marker proteins and so at least one of our modalities contains some known structure which should be incorporated into the modelling. This motivates developing semi-supervised integrative methodology and account for dataset-specific models. Our approach is applicable beyond MS-based spatial proteomics to general semi-supervised integrative tasks (such as the integration of clinical measurements with genomic data in personalized medicine applications) and is implemented in an R-package: <kbd>mdir</kbd> available from <a href="https://github.com/stcolema/MDIr" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://github.com/stcolema/MDIr</a>.</p>
<p>The paper is organised as follows. In the <a href="#sec002" class="usa-link">Materials and methods</a> section the models used and data analysed in this paper are presented. This includes a description of the simulation study comparing some of the models, the datasets used to validate our approach on real data and our case study, two ’omics datasets for the model apicomplexan, <em>Toxoplasma gondii</em>. The <a href="#sec015" class="usa-link">Results</a> section deals with the results from analysing these data showing the advantage of leveraging information from observed markers/labels and across datasets via integrative modelling. In the analysis of <em>T. Gondii</em>, we find evidence for dividing the dense granule proteins into heterogeneous populations with potentially different functions. Finally, in <a href="#sec020" class="usa-link">Discussion</a> we consider the results described in preceding sections.</p></section><section id="sec002"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Materials and methods</h2>
<section id="sec003"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods</h3>
<p>We introduce Gaussian mixture models as the foundation of the TAGM [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref023" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref023">23</a>], GP mixture and (multiple dataset integration) MDI [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref040">40</a>] models, which we then introduce in turn. Finally we introduce the KNN transfer learning algorithm, a supervised method for multi-modal data in spatial proteomics.</p>
<section id="sec004"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Mixture models.</h4>
<p>Let <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e001"><math id="M1" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>X</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> be the observed data, with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e002"><math id="M2" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>P</mi></mrow></msub><msup><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>⊤</mo></msup></mrow></math></span> for each item being considered, where each observation has <em>P</em> variables. We wish to model the data using a mixture of densities:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e003"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M3" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>θ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>θ</mi><mi>K</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>π</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>K</mi></munderover><msub><mi>π</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>θ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>

The component weights are restricted to the unit simplex, i.e., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e008"><math id="M8" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>K</mi></msubsup><msub><mi>π</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>. To capture the discrete structure in the data, we introduce an allocation variable, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e009"><math id="M9" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>c</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><msup><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>⊤</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>∈</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>K</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>⊂</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow></math></span>, to indicate which component a sample was drawn from, introducing conditional independence between the components,</p>

<td class="label">(9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <em>F</em> is the appropriate distribution (e.g., Gaussian, Categorical, etc.) and <em>G</em><sup>(0)</sup> is some prior over the component parameters. In practice, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e018"><math id="M18" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>θ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> represents the parameters characterizing each cellular compartment (e.g., mean protein abundance profiles), <em>π</em> captures the relative sizes of compartments, and <em>c</em><sub><em>n</em></sub> indicates which compartment each protein belongs to. The hierarchical structure allows uncertainty to propagate through all levels of the model. We use the empirical priors suggested by Fraley and Raftery [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref043" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref043">43</a>] for the Gaussian mixture models. For the Categorical model we use the average proportion of each class for each feature to define our prior density.</p></section><section id="sec005"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">T-augmented Gaussian mixture model.</h4>
<p>Crook et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref023" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref023">23</a>] proposed capturing outliers within the Gaussian mixture model using a heavy-tailed multivariate t (<strong>MVT</strong>) distribution in their t-augmented Gaussian mixture (<strong>TAGM</strong>) model. They consider each component a mixture of a multivariate normal (<strong>MVN</strong>) density and this MVT which has common parameters across all components, i.e.,</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e019"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M19" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><mo>·</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo fence="true" form="prefix" stretchy="true">{</mo><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>M</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>η</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mtext>if the</mtext><mi> </mi><msup><mi>n</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">th</mi></msup><mi> </mi><mi mathvariant="normal">item is an outlier</mi></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><msub><mi>f</mi><mrow><mi>𝒩</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mo>Σ</mo><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mtext>otherwise</mtext><mo>,</mo></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(10)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e020"><math id="M20" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e021"><math id="M21" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mo>Σ</mo><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> are the mean vector and covariance matrix of the <em>k</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> component, and <em>M</em>,<em>V</em> and <em>η</em> are the parameters of the MVT which are set empirically and not sampled. Being considered an outlier is an inferred quantity indicated through the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e022"><math id="M22" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>ψ</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mi>ψ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>p</mi><mi>s</mi><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><msup><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>⊤</mo></msup></mrow></math></span> binary variable. Note that the items of known label (in our applications, the marker proteins) are never considered outliers. This extends the hierarchical model described above to:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e023"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M23" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>ψ</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mo>Σ</mo><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>~</mi><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mo>Σ</mo><mi>k</mi></msub><msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mrow><msub><mi>ψ</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></msup><mi>𝒯</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>η</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>M</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>V</mi><msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>ψ</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(11)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e024"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M24" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>π</mi><mi>~</mi><mtext>Categorical</mtext><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>π</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>π</mi><mi>K</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(12)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e025"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M25" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>ψ</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>ϵ</mi><mi>~</mi><mtext>Bernoulli</mtext><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mi>ϵ</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(13)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e026"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M26" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>π</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>π</mi><mi>K</mi></msub><mi>~</mi><mtext>Dirichlet</mtext><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>K</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>K</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(14)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e027"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M27" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mo>Σ</mo><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>~</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="script">N</mi><mi mathvariant="script">I</mi><mi mathvariant="script">W</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>μ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>κ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>ν</mi><mo>,</mo><mo>Ψ</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(15)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where we set <em>η</em> to 4 to ensure heavy-tails, and set <em>M</em> to empirical mean and <em>V</em> to half of the global variance of the data following Crook et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref023" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref023">23</a>].</p></section><section id="sec006"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Gaussian process.</h4>
<p>The Gaussian Process (<strong>GP</strong>) is a generalisation of the Gaussian probability distribution over functions or infinite vectors rather than finite vectors. The GP is immensely flexible and can describe measurements of arbitrary proximity, most relevant in modelling temporal or spatial data. Specifically, the GP is a continuous stochastic process with index set <em>T</em> such that its realisation at any finite collection of points <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e028"><math id="M28" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>P</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo><mo>⊂</mo><mi>T</mi></mrow></math></span> is jointly Gaussian. It is uniquely specified by a mean function <em>m</em>(<em>t</em>) and a positive semi-definite covariance kernel <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e029"><math id="M29" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>·</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>·</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>, which determine the mean vectors and covariance matrices of the associated multivariate Gaussian distributions. That is, <em>f</em>(<em>x</em>) is a GP with mean function <em>m</em>(<em>t</em>) and covariance kernel <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e030"><math id="M30" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>·</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>·</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> if for any <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e031"><math id="M31" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>P</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo><mo>⊂</mo><mi>T</mi></mrow></math></span></p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e032"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M32" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>P</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>~</mi><mi>𝒩</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>P</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>Σ</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(16)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e033"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M33" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mo>Σ</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(17)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>GP models have been used in a range of problems such as systems biology analyses [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref044" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref044">44</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref048" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref048">48</a>] environmental and health studies [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref049" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref049">49</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref050">50</a>], and monitoring patient health [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref051" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref051">51</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref052" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref052">52</a>] and are built upon rigorous theoretical foundations in terms of support and contraction rates [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref053" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref053">53</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref055">55</a>].</p>
<p>In practice, a prior mean function of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e034"><math id="M34" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span> is commonly used and the covariance function is chosen from a small family of easy to implement functions such as the squared exponential function [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref056" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref056">56</a>]. Lacking any strong belief about periodicity, symmetry or other constraints on the functions, we use the squared exponential function which is defined for two time points/locations <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e035"><math id="M35" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>t</mi><mi>′</mi></msup></mrow></math></span>, as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e036"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M36" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>t</mi><mi>′</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><msup><mi>a</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mi>exp</mi><mrow><mo fence="true" form="prefix" stretchy="true">{</mo><mo>−</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>t</mi><mi>′</mi></msup><msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><msup><mi>l</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mfrac><mo fence="true" form="postfix" stretchy="true">}</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(18)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <em>a</em> and <em>l</em> are the amplitude and length-scale respectively. We use the most common choice for the metric <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e037"><math id="M37" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>·</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>·</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>, Euclidean distance or the <em>l</em><sup>2</sup>-norm.</p></section><section id="sec007"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Gaussian process mixture models.</h4>
<p>If we consider the mean vector of a Gaussian density as the finite realisation of an infinite function in our feature space, we can place a Gaussian process prior over the space of functions it is drawn from. Thus, for the <em>k</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> component of this mixture, mean vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e038"><math id="M38" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> and observed data which has been transformed to a vector, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e039"><math id="M39" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></msub></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>,</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e040"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M40" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>σ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mi>~</mi><mi>𝒩</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>σ</mi><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>P</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(19)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M41" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mi>~</mi><mi>G</mi><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(20)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>As we assume exchangeable data for ease of notation we permute the original dataset such that we can denote <em>X</em><sub><em>k</em></sub> with contiguous indices, i.e., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e042"><math id="M42" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>. The posterior distribution can be derived by using the properties of the multivariate Gaussian relating to conditional distribution of two jointly distributed Gaussian variables [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref057" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref057">57</a>],</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e043"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M43" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo>[</mo><mtable><mtr><mtd><mi>y</mi></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd></mtr></mtable><mo>]</mo><mi>~</mi><mi>𝒩</mi><mrow><mo fence="true" form="prefix" stretchy="true">(</mo><mrow><mn mathvariant="bold">0</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mo>[</mo><mtable><mtr><mtd><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>X</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mi>𝐈</mi></mtd><mtd><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>X</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>X</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd><mtd><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd></mtr></mtable><mo>]</mo><mo fence="true" form="postfix" stretchy="true">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(21)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e044"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M44" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>⟹</mi><mrow><mo fence="true" form="prefix" stretchy="true">{</mo><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mi>𝔼</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mo>=</mo><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>X</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>X</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mi>𝐈</mi><msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mi>y</mi><mo>,</mo></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mi>ℂ</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mo>=</mo><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>−</mo><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>X</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>X</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mi>𝐈</mi><msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mi>C</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>X</mi><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>X</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>.</mo></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(22)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>We can then say</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e045"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M45" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>μ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>σ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>~</mi><mi>G</mi><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>m</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mover><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">~</mo></mover><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(23)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e046"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M46" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>m</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>:</mo><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mi>σ</mi><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><msub><mi>I</mi><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>K</mi></msub><mi>P</mi></mrow></msub><msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(24)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e047"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M47" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mover><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">~</mo></mover><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>:</mo><mi>P</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>:</mo><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>:</mo><mi>P</mi><mo>,</mo><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mi>σ</mi><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><msub><mi>I</mi><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>K</mi></msub><mi>P</mi></mrow></msub><msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>:</mo><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(25)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>This involves an expensive calculation, taking the inverse of an <em>N</em><sub><em>k</em></sub><em>P</em> × <em>N</em><sub><em>k</em></sub><em>P</em> matrix. There are methods to scale GP models, e.g., sparse approximations [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref058" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref058">58</a>] and low-dimensional approximations [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref059" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref059">59</a>], see Liu et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref060" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref060">60</a>] for a review of the subject. However, in our application we have rich structure in the data as all items within a modality have the same time/spatial measurements with consistent distance between each measurement and we can use this to reduce the computational load without using any of these more complex methods [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref061" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref061">61</a>], we can instead rewrite the posterior parameters as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e048"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M48" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>m</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>σ</mi><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow></mfrac><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>×</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msup><mi>Q</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mover><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="true">¯</mo></mover><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(26)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e049"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M49" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mover><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">~</mo></mover><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>σ</mi><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow></mfrac><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>×</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msup><mi>Q</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(27)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Please see section A.1 in S1 Text for this derivation.</p>
<p>We sample the hyper-parameters <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e050"><math id="M50" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>a</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>l</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> according to a Metropolis-Hastings scheme. Standard log-normal hyperpriors were used.</p></section><section id="sec008"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Multiple dataset integration.</h4>
<p>MDI is a Bayesian integrative or multi-modal clustering method. Bayesian statistics allows quantification of uncertainty through the use of probability distributions and offers a modular framework for data analysis by making dependencies between data and parameters explicit [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref062" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref062">62</a>]. In MDI, signal sharing is defined by the prior on the cluster label of the <em>n</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> observation in the <em>M</em> modalities/datasets:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e051"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M51" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>M</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mo>·</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∏</mo><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></munderover><msubsup><mi>γ</mi><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><munderover><mo>∏</mo><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></munderover><munderover><mo>∏</mo><mrow><mi>l</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></munderover><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>l</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msub><mi>𝕀</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>l</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(28)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e052"><math id="M52" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup></mrow></math></span> is the label of the <em>n</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> observation in the <em>m</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> modality, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e053"><math id="M53" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>γ</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup></mrow></math></span> is the weight of the <em>k</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> cluster in the <em>m</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> modality, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e054"><math id="M54" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>l</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> is the similarity between the clusterings of the <em>m</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> and <em>l</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> modalities and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e055"><math id="M55" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>𝕀</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> is the indicator function returning 1 if <em>x</em> is true and 0 otherwise. Attractively, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e056"><math id="M56" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>l</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> is inferred from the data and if there is no shared signal it will tend towards 0 giving us no dependency between these modalities in which case each dataset will have independent clusters.</p>
<p>Within each dataset/modality MDI uses a specific density to model the probability of allocation to a specific cluster. For example, one might wish to model continuous data with Gaussian densities, categorical data with Categorical distributions and/or time-series data with Gaussian processes. Thus we have flexibility in how we model each dataset within the MDI framework.</p>
<p>We implement this in C++ with the novel functionality to allow for observed classes and labels in a subset of datasets/modes and make this available through the R package <kbd>MDIr</kbd>. In the Bayesian paradigm, moving from an unsupervised cluster method to a semi-supervised method can be phrased as a missing data problem. Let <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e057"><math id="M57" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>I</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></math></span> be a vector indicating if the label <em>c</em><sub><em>n</em></sub> is observed or not. If <em>i</em><sub><em>n</em></sub> = 0 (i.e. the label is unobserved), then <em>c</em><sub><em>n</em></sub> is sampled as usual, but if <em>i</em><sub><em>n</em></sub> = 1 then <em>c</em><sub><em>n</em></sub> is known and held fixed. Observing labels means that we can no longer arbitrarily switch labels as the labels are inherently linked to a specific class (in our case, subcellular niches). The Gibbs sampler to perform inference on the model is implemented in C++ and includes a correction to the conditional distribution of the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e058"><math id="M58" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>ϕ</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>l</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> parameters which was incorrect in previous implementations as observed by Stephen Johnson, Daniel Henderson, and Richard Boys in 2017 (private correspondence; see section A.2 of S1 Text).</p>
<p>As per Kirk et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref040">40</a>], we use an overfitted mixture model [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref063" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref063">63</a>] to approximate a Dirichlet process mixture model [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref064" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref064">64</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref067" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref067">67</a>] in each unsupervised modality, allowing inference of the number of clusters/classes present. If a number of classes are observed but one believes that additional classes might be present in the data one may use a semi-supervised overfitted mixture to detect novel classes as per Crook et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref068" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref068">68</a>].</p></section><section id="sec009"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Markov chain Monte Carlo methods.</h4>
<p>We use Markov chain Monte Carlo (<strong>MCMC</strong>) methods [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref069">69</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref072" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref072">72</a>] to perform inference on our Bayesian models. MCMC is the gold standard for Computational Bayes [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref073" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref073">73</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref076" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref076">76</a>], constructing a Markov chain of sampled parameters to describe the conditional posterior density for model parameters, with asymptotic guarantees that this description is exact. However, in practice MCMC is prone to becoming trapped in local modes of the target density. Designing algorithms that perform better in this scenario is an area of active research [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref077" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref077">77</a>–<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref083" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref083">83</a>], but implementing these methods can be time-consuming and with no guarantee of overcoming the problem. We instead use Gibbs sampling [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref084" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref084">84</a>] in our implementation of MDI and the consensus clustering approach to circumvent multi-modality [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref085" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref085">85</a>]. We can use the collection of sampled values to infer a point estimate and provide a quantification of uncertainty about this. Common choices of point estimate for continuous parameters are the median or the mean, but to obtain a summary clustering we instead use that which minimises the variation of information [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref086" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref086">86</a>], which has been found to have desirable properties [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref087" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref087">87</a>], though alternative approaches exist [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref088" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref088">88</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref089" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref089">89</a>]. To summarise the uncertainty about a partition, the posterior similarity matrix (<strong>PSM</strong>) is often used. This considers the average co-clustering probability of each pair of items based on the sampled clusterings. It is an <em>N</em> × <em>N</em> symmetric matrix. For a classification, we assign the samples to the class with the highest average probability of allocation across the retained MCMC samples.</p></section><section id="sec010"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Transfer learning using a <em>k</em>-nearest neighbours framework.</h4>
<p>The <em>k</em>-nearest neighbours (<strong>KNN</strong>) classifier is a supervised method in which predictions are made using the majority vote of an item’s <em>k</em> nearest labelled neighbours. The KNN-transfer learning (<strong>KNN-TL</strong>) classifier extends this to transfer information from an auxiliary modality to a primary modality [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref041">41</a>,<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref090" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref090">90</a>]. To use the transfer learning algorithm, the best choice of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e059"><math id="M59" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></math></span>, the number of neighbours considered when classifying items with missing labels for each modality, must be estimated from the KNN classifier to be used as inputs into the KNN-TL classifier. This is performed via a grid search on a pre-specified grid, with the optimal value selected through cross-validation. Breckels et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref041">41</a>] adapted this method to predict protein localisation using LOPIT data as the primary dataset and GO terms as the auxiliary dataset (in contrast to MDI, the KNN-TL classifier does consider an ordering of datasets). In this context, this algorithm finds the <em>k</em><sub>1</sub> nearest marker proteins to the <em>n</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> protein in the LOPIT data and the <em>k</em><sub>2</sub> nearest marker proteins in the GO data. The representation of each class among these neighbours is calculated, i.e., if <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e060"><math id="M60" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>Z</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>Z</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></math></span> are the sets of neighbours in the first and second modalities respectively,</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e061"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M61" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mi>j</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>Z</mi><mi>v</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mfrac><munder><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>∈</mo><msub><mi>Z</mi><mi>v</mi></msub></mrow></munder><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mi>j</mi><mo>,</mo><mtext>for</mtext><mi> </mi><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>K</mi><mtext mathvariant="italic">(v)</mtext></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(29)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>from which a localisation is predicted</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e062"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M62" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>j</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>θ</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mi>j</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>θ</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mi>j</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(30)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e063"><math id="M63" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>θ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span> is estimated using a grid search (again, the optimal value is selected through cross-validation on the marker proteins). The final estimate is <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e064"><math id="M64" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mover><mrow><mi>c</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">^</mo></mover><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>arg</mi><msub><mo>max</mo><mi>j</mi></msub><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>j</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>.</p></section></section><section id="sec011"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Materials</h3>
<section id="sec012"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Simulation study.</h4>
<p>Our simulation study investigates the benefit of having observed labels available in a modality (such as marker proteins in a hyperLOPIT experiment), and the advantage of performing an integrative analysis rather than independent analyses of each modality. To achieve this, we construct three scenarios. Each scenario is defined by the density used to generate the clusters (the generative model) and by the <em>ϕ</em> vector which determines the similarity of the clustering structure across modalities. We first generate the labels indicating the cluster an item is generated from in each modality and then sample measurements based on this cluster’s parameters. In every simulation, the first dataset is generated from six clusters, the second from seven clusters and the third from eight clusters. We generate two hundred labels and data points in each datasets from this model:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e065"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M65" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mi>k</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>γ</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>ϕ</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>∝</mo><msub><mi>γ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><munderover><mo>∏</mo><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mi>;</mi><mi>w</mi><mo>≠</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mi>V</mi></munderover><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(31)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="pcbi.1013799.e066"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M66" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mi>k</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>~</mi><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>θ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(32)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>There are fifteen measurements/feature for each data point (i.e., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e067"><math id="M67" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>200</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>P</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>15</mn></mrow></math></span> in each dataset in all simulations). For the semi-supervised models, a random 30% of the labels are given as observed in the first dataset with no labels or classes observed in the second and third datasets in all models. Within a generating seed the labels in each modality are the same across all scenarios and each dataset is informative of the others (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e068"><math id="M68" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>ϕ</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>12</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>8</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>4</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>). The Adjusted Rand Index (<strong>ARI</strong>) [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref091" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref091">91</a>] between the true labels of each pair of datasets across simulations is shown in <a href="#pcbi.1013799.g001" class="usa-link">Fig 1A</a>. The ARI is a score comparing two partitions, adjusted for chance. A value of 0 means two partitions are no more similar than a pair of random partitions would be expected to be, 1 means they are identical.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="pcbi.1013799.g001"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig 1. A. ARI between the generating labels for each combination of dataset pairings within a given simulation across all scenarios.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12721539_pcbi.1013799.g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a1f2/12721539/a1e1ffc99f1e/pcbi.1013799.g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="330" width="746" alt="Fig 1"></a></p>

<p>Scenario 3, the Log-Poisson case, is more complex.</p>

<td class="label">(37)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>This is inspired by the simulation study of Chandra et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref092" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref092">92</a>], but the Gaussian noise is added after the log-transform to ensure this transform is always possible. Each cluster strongly deviates from Gaussian in this case. An example of the simulated data is shown in <a href="#pcbi.1013799.g001" class="usa-link">Fig 1B</a>. These distributions were selected to test robustness under different model assumptions: Gaussian represents ideal conditions matching model assumptions (often assumed for gene co-expression data), MVT tests heavy-tailed outlier scenarios common in proteomics, and Log-Poisson evaluates performance under severe distributional misspecification (based on log-normalization of count data such as RNA-seq). A full description of the generating mechanisms and choice of parameters to differentiate the datasets are given in section B of S1 Text.</p>
<p>We fit five different models in each simulation. These are distinguished with the information available to them in the first dataset and whether they jointly model the datasets (i.e., MDI) or model each dataset separately using a mixture model, and if the model has access to class labels and the true number of classes present (e.g., when the number of subcellular niches is unknown). This comparison allows us to isolate the benefits of: (1) integrative vs. independent modeling, (2) supervised vs. unsupervised learning, and (3) known vs. inferred cluster numbers. In all cases datasets 2 and 3 are unsupervised and the number of clusters present is inferred. For the overfitted approaches, we use the overfitted mixture model framework of [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref063" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref063">63</a>], which includes more mixture components than expected and allows the data to determine the effective number of clusters through Bayesian model selection. This creates two variants of semi-supervised MDI: (1) semi-supervised MDI where the true number of subcellular compartments is provided as prior knowledge, and (2) overfitted semi-supervised MDI where this number must be inferred from the data alone. The overfitted approach is often relevant to real applications where the total number of subcellular niches may be unknown, while the standard approach provides an upper bound on performance when complete prior knowledge is available.</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>Unsupervised MDI: MDI with no observed labels and the number of clusters unknown,</p></li>
<li><p>Overfitted semi-supervised MDI: MDI with known labels, but the true number of clusters is hidden,</p></li>
<li><p>Semi-supervised MDI: MDI with both observed labels and the true number of clusters known,</p></li>
<li><p>Overfitted semi-supervised mixture model: mixture model with known labels, but the true number of clusters is hidden,</p></li>
<li><p>Semi-supervised mixture model: mixture model with both observed labels and the true number of clusters known,</p></li>
</ul>
<p>We run 10 chains of each method to each simulated dataset. Each chain is 15,000 iterations long with the first 7,500 discarded as burn-in. We thin to every 100th sample to reduce autocorrelation. The best chain for the MDI methods is determined by the chain with the highest mean ARI between the point estimate clustering and the truth across datasets whereas for the mixture model the chains are selected based on the highest ARI between the point estimate and the truth in each dataset (rather than using the same seed across datasets).</p></section><section id="sec013"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Validation study.</h4>
<p>We compare our approach to a state-of-the-art transfer learning method previously applied to LOPIT data, the KNN-TL classifier, using the marker proteins from four of the same datasets analysed by Breckels et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref041">41</a>]. This includes two datasets for <em>Arabidopsis thaliana</em>, the <em>Root Callus</em> [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref016" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref016">16</a>] dataset (referred to hereafter as <em>Callus</em> to distinguish it from the second <em>A. thaliana</em> dataset) which contains 261 marker proteins and 16 fractions, and the <em>Root</em> dataset [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref093" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref093">93</a>] which contains 185 marker proteins and six fractions. a dataset for human embryonic kidney fibroblast cells [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref094" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref094">94</a>] which contains 404 marker proteins and 8 fractions, and a mouse pluripotent stem cell dataset [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref012" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref012">12</a>] which contains 8 fractions for 387 marker proteins. These are available through the <kbd>pRolocdata</kbd> Bioconductor package. <a href="#pcbi.1013799.g002" class="usa-link">Fig 2</a> shows PCA plots of these proteins. These are paired with GO term data. GO terms record observed and inferred characteristics of gene and gene products in terms of three sub-ontologies. The cellular component (<strong>CC</strong>) the product localises to; its molecular function (<strong>MF</strong>), such as binding or catalysis; and the biological processes (<strong>BP</strong>) the product is involved, e.g., chromatin binding. The data we use are the same as are available through the <kbd>pRolocdata</kbd> Bioconductor package [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref095" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref095">95</a>]. This data was prepared by Breckels et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref041">41</a>] by using all possible GO CC terms associated to the proteins in the experiment to construct a binary matrix representing the presence/absence of a given term for each protein, for each experiment.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="pcbi.1013799.g002"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig 2. The <em>T. gondii</em> data.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12721539_pcbi.1013799.g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a1f2/12721539/8eb68c1ad9fb/pcbi.1013799.g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="580" width="749" alt="Fig 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/pcbi.1013799.g002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>A) A tSNE of the hyperLOPIT data from Baryluk et al. with the marker proteins of each organelle highlighted. B) The gene expression data from Behnke et al. C) <a href="#pcbi.1013799.g001" class="usa-link">Fig 1</a> from Behnke et al. showing progression of cell number per vacuole (vacuole size, squares) and portion of cells undergoing division (triangles) at different timepoints in the gene expression data.</p></figcaption></figure><p>We model the proteomic datasets with a TAGM model and the GO terms using a categorical mixture model (see the supplementary material of [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref040">40</a>] for a description of this model) within the MDI framework. We run five chains of the TAGM and MDI models for 15,000 iterations, discard the first 5,000 as warm-up, thin to every 50th and then combine samples across chains (i.e. construct an ensemble). We follow the vignette of Breckels et al. [<a href="#pcbi.1013799.ref094" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="pcbi.1013799.ref094">94</a>] for the KNN-TL algorithm. This involves identifying the inputs <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e074"><math id="M74" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></math></span> and <em>θ</em> in a grid search using cross-validation. We consider a range of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e075"><math id="M75" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>5</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>7</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>9</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>11</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>13</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>15</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>17</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>19</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e076"><math id="M76" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>θ</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0.0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.25</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.50</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.75</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1.0</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e077"><math id="M77" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>…</mi><mi>K</mi></mrow></math></span>. As all the combinations of <em>θ</em> must be considered this becomes computationally intensive as <em>K</em> increases. We considered the smaller of either the full combination matrix or a random sample of 20,000 rows. We inspect performance across ten folds with approximately 30% of the marker proteins observed in each organelle and 70% of the localisations predicted.</p>
<p>We compare performance using the Brier loss, macro F1 score and accuracy (defined below). Accuracy is the number of predictions that match the ground truth divided by the total number of predictions; this is the simplest score we use as it does not account for either uncertainty or class size. The F1 score is a common measure of performance in binary classification examples that corrects for sensitivity and specificity. The variation considered here is an extension to the multi-class setting. Letting <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="pcbi.1013799.e078"><math id="M78" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mover><mrow><mi>c</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">^</mo></mover><mi>n</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> denote the predicted class for the <em>n</em><sup><em>th</em></sup> item and <em>c</em><sub><em>n</em></sub> denote the true class, then</p>
